S U S T A I N A B I L I T Y  | T H E M A T I C  R E S E A R C H  | O C T O B E R  2 0 2 6
The report outlines the top 10 focus topics, discussions and outcomes for COP31 (see pages 1-3 for a summary)

### Page 2

C O P 3 1  P r ev iew  |  TO P  1 0  (1  o f 3 )
Antalya  - 10 things to watch for at COP31
2 Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
From 9 to 20 November, ~100,000 participants and nearly 200 Parties are expected at COP31 in Antalya, Türkiye. This is an ‘Implementation COP’ and will focus on climate finance delivery for
mitigation and adaptation commitments. Yes, there will be geopolitical tensions and competitiveness concerns. But, for corporates and investors, the discussions will shape how private capital is

mobilised, and how policies will affect de-risking efforts. We expect electrification efforts will feature prominently, driven by energy security concerns as well as by rising AI-driven power
demand. Furthermore, AI’s ability to help tackle climate change has the potential to play a bigger role in shaping the agenda of future COPs.
01
TÜRKIYE HOSTS, AUSTRALIA LEADS
NEGOTIATIONS
Türkiye and Australia must stay aligned
to drive progress
See page 6
– As host, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum will preside over COP31 and its Action Agenda, which is intended to build cooperation between

willing participants to drive climate action. Australia’s Chris Bowen will run the formal negotiations that will seek consensus (or
lack of objection) on finance delivery and existing climate commitments. Feedback following pre-COP meetings on the
arrangement has been broadly positive, but concern remains over how any potential disagreement will be handled.
– This structure is the result of a compromise after both countries bid to host. Both countries want COP31 to focus on climate

finance implementation and clean-energy growth, however, they remain coal-reliant (in different ways) with no national coal
phase-out date, a criticism likely to continue from climate advocates at COP.
02
CLIMATE FINANCE
Success at COP31 requires notable
progress on mobilising existing
commitments
See page 7
– For COP31 to be successful it will need to deliver agreements to mobilise and channel climate finance. For context, at COP29 in
2024 it was agreed (as part of formal negotiations) that developed nations would commit $300bn in climate finance annually

by 2035, with a call for $1.3tn from all sources. At present, more credible plans are needed to secure existing commitments
and attract broader capital. Note the EU has already stated it will not make new finance pledges at COP31.
– Article 9 contains the main financing provisions of the Paris Agreement and has driven much debate during COP talks.
Developing countries want focus on public funding from developed governments (Article 9.1), while developed countries prefer

to discuss Article 9 in a broader context. This allows private capital to be included. COP30 launched a two-year work
programme on Article 9, but June’s Bonn talks stalled, so COP31 meetings will attempt to reach agreement.
03
ADAPTATION & RESILIENCE
Implications of a 1.5°C overshoot point
to more adaptation financing
See pages 8–9
– The UN has warned that an overshoot of the Paris Agreement’s 1.5°C goal is now unavoidable, at least temporarily. That has

dampened morale for climate supporters ahead of COP but, at the same time, it has drawn more attention to the topic of
adaptation finance, which is set to be on the formal negotiating agenda. Parties appear deadlocked over whether the COP30
goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035 (potential annual target of $120bn) should be explicitly referenced in the final COP31
decision on the Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA). This is important, as anchoring it to the GGA could signal finance flows.

– The EU Commission proposed the Climate Resilience law on 27 October. It needs Parliament and Council approval, but if
approved, member states will be required to run national climate-risk assessments based on a 3°C-by-2100 warming scenario
(i.e. the EU is planning for overshoot). Members would also need to adopt resilience plans, including details of costs and
funding, with The Commission estimating the EU needs €70bn a year for adaptation to 2050, vs. €29bn of current spend.

### Page 3

Antalya  - 10 things to watch for at COP31
3 Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
04
ELECTRIFICATION
Türkiye to champion voluntary
electrification commitments
See page 10
– Türkiye's voluntary Action Agenda includes a ‘35-by-35’ target to lift electricity’s share of final energy use from ~23% to 35% by
2035, alongside waste, building energy intensity and circularity targets. This sits outside the formal negotiations and commits
only those that volunteer. China and the EU have voiced support, and the timing is right given global energy-security concerns,

though some question the emissions impact if fossil fuels are used to generate the additional electricity.
06
TRADE TENSIONS
Trade’s inclusion in the provisional
agenda is contentious, with the
potential for disagreement over trade to
stall progress on wider agreements
See page 12
– The role of trade at COP is contentious, but China wants it discussed. On 21 October, Vice Minister Li Gao called ‘unilateralism
and protectionism’ a ‘new problem’ undermining collective climate efforts. Specifically, he wants COP31 to discuss the free

flow of green technology. This was widely read as being aimed at US and EU tariffs on Chinese cleantech.
– Despite a COP30 agreement to create three annual trade dialogues in 2026–28 (the first formal space under the UNFCCC to
discuss trade) away from COP, developing countries are still pushing for trade to be a formal negotiating item in Antalya, and it
is on the provisional agenda. They argue that climate-related trade measures such as the CBAM burden their exporters and

question whether they are compatible with UNFCCC principles. The EU meanwhile claims they help prevent carbon leakage,
with developed countries preferring trade discussions to focus on technical information-sharing without duplicating the WTO.
C O P 3 1  P r ev iew  |  TO P  1 0  (2  o f 3 )
07
GEOPOLITICS & MULTILATERALISM
Consensus under strain, but ‘coalitions
of the willing’ are rising
See pages 13–14
– Geopolitical fragmentation and the US exit from the Paris Agreement make consensus, historically central to COP decision-

making, harder among nearly 200 nations. This has encouraged more voluntary action and “coalitions of the willing” to pursue
more ambitious goals. If these strategies deliver more effective progress, their approach will shape future COPs.
05
FOSSIL FUELS
Phase-out likely not on the formal
agenda, but workarounds are emerging
See page 11
– Brazil’s COP30 presidency is to present a fossil fuel transition roadmap separately, outside the UN formal negotiating process.

For perspective, COP28 in Dubai reached consensus on “transitioning away from fossil fuels in energy systems”, but at COP30
in Belém (2025) calls from more than 80 countries for a transition roadmap faced opposition from other nations, so it was
omitted from the final decision and moved to a separate track.
– Santa Marta is a second workaround for the lack of formal agreement on a transition roadmap. The Colombia- and
Netherlands-led process is a ‘coalition of the willing’ of 57 participants (excluding the US, China, India and Russia) agreeing to

voluntary action to move away from fossil fuels faster than consensus allows. At COP31, Colombia and the Netherlands want
to bring the Santa Marta outcomes into the summit. The format is not yet settled, but the Australian and Turkish presidencies
have said they are open to it. The process then heads to a second conference in Tuvalu, co-hosted with Ireland, in 2027.

### Page 4

09
NATURE & CARBON CREDITS
Efforts to boost demand and build
credibility to help scale carbon markets
See page 16
– Carbon credit discussions will centre on government policies to boost demand and corporate engagement, with much of the
formal UN architecture now in place. If carbon markets can scale, the benefits would be wide-ranging (e.g. supporting
emissions targets and providing finance for developing countries). Credibility and greenwashing concerns remain key risks,

however; we expect prices to diverge in favour of high-integrity credits.
– The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30, plans a fixed-income portfolio to reward verified forest
conservation. It now has ~$7.8bn of sponsor capital against a $10bn end-2026 target. In our view, fresh COP31 commitments
would signal appetite to fund nature conservation and protection.
10
PACIFIC, OCEANS & SEA-LEVEL RISE
Australia to bring Pacific priorities into

focus
See page 17
– Australia asked for a Pacific pre-COP in return for ceding the hosting. It has promised to keep championing Pacific interests at
COP31. The Taku Pakasoa Declaration was adopted at that pre-COP on 6 October. ‘Taku Pakasoa’ is Tuvaluan for strength
found in unity and collective action. It is a joint call to keep 1.5°C alive, calling for action on climate finance, resilience and
oceans. Its intention is to give the Pacific a shared position going into COP31 and signal its policy priorities to other blocs.

Other countries are invited to sign on. The climate-ocean nexus will also be a COP31 focus, including investable solutions.
COP31 should indicate what COP can deliver in a more fragmented geopolitical world. Key questions? Can the current structure help mobilise meaningful finance and investment, and do
smaller coalitions and voluntary commitments deliver meaningful outcomes? Answers here could shape future COP agendas.  Looking ahead, Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027. There, we

expect a continued focus on delivering on commitments and improving access to climate finance, reflecting African priorities. COP33 then falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, with China
reportedly considering a bid and South Korea potentially interested.
We should note that the second Global Stocktake, the Paris Agreement’s five-yearly review of collective progress, begins at COP31 and concludes at COP33, with a technical assessment
(2027) informing the final negotiations. Its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

08
AI & CLIMATE
AI’s power demand in focus, with the
Antalya pledge calling for low emissions
infrastructure
See page 15
– The Antalya AI Pledge invites governments and companies to commit voluntarily to sustainable infrastructure and
environmental transparency. Its call for zero- and low-emissions energy infrastructure leaves room for nuclear power,
prompting discussion of its role in meeting data-centre demand alongside renewables. While we see binding commitments as

unlikely, watch for corporate power commitments and technology partnerships.
– We expect AI’s climate potential and rising electricity demand to feature prominently at COP31, prompting debate over data-
centre energy use. We also expect AI to help delegations manage COP’s heavy paperwork, from reviewing negotiating texts
and submissions to comparing successive draft decisions. This should help negotiating teams prepare more effectively and
make decisions faster, particularly smaller delegations with limited staff and resources.

### Page 6

TO P I C  1 | COP31’s UNIQUE STRUCTURE
COP31’s unique dual structure will need to bridge divides among negotiating groups and build consensus
As host, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum will preside over COP31 and its Action Agenda. Australia’s Chris Bowen will run the formal negotiations. This dual structure is the result of a compromise after
both countries bid to host. Feedback post pre-COP meetings on the unique arrangement has been broadly positive, but concern remains over how any disagreement between the two countries

would be handled should it occur. Both countries clearly want this year’s COP to focus on climate finance implementation and clean-energy growth.
A unique leadership structure
COP31 will take place in Antalya, Türkiye. The setup is unusual, emerging as part of a deal
between Türkiye and Australia after both countries bid to host the event. What began as a
compromise could become an advantage, provided the two countries remain aligned. Their
differing diplomatic relationships could help them bridge divides between negotiating groups

and build common ground on climate action and finance.
How it will work
Murat Kurum, Türkiye’s Minister of the Environment, will serve as COP31 President, overseeing
the conference and Action Agenda. Australia’s Minister for Climate Change and Energy, Chris
Bowen, will serve as President of Negotiations, including drafting final texts, with authority to
steer government-to-government talks. Pre-COP preparations have reportedly been positive.
They will, however, both likely face criticism from climate advocates asking for a plan to shift

from fossil fuels, notably coal, given efforts to position themselves as global climate leaders.
The Action Agenda includes targets for electrification, waste, buildings and circularity
This voluntary Agenda proposes 2035 targets of a 35% electricity share of final energy use (up
from 23% currently), halving projected municipal waste growth, cutting building energy
intensity by at least 25% and reaching at least 15% circular material use. While pledges are

non-binding, they can signal policy and decisions that could support infrastructure investment.
Formal negotiations to focus on implementing existing commitments
Formal negotiations seek consensus (or lack of objection) on implementation pathways. The
key need is guidance on how agreed climate-finance commitments will be funded and
delivered, and on countries’ roles. Adaptation will also be a focus, with countries aiming to
reach agreement on how to advance the Global Goal on Adaptation after discussions stalled

earlier this year. These formal talks remain separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.
Sources: COP31 Action Agenda, IEA and Eco-Business

### Page 7

TO P I C  2 | C L I MATE F I N AN C E
Climate finance delivery will be a central test for COP31
June’s Bonn preparatory talks reportedly made little progress and exposed persistent finance divisions, leaving COP31 to turn both the $300bn and $1.3tn-by-2035 goals into a more credible
delivery plan. This is a big ask and requires clearer public/private roles, consistent tracking mechanisms, more concessional finance, risk-sharing and guarantees that can help reduce the cost of

capital. New large-scale finance commitments appear unlikely, with the EU already stating it will not make new pledges.
7
Climate finance: needs, commitments and progress

COP31 focus: first implementation update on the Baku-Belem
roadmap to $1.3T. This total is inclusive of the $300bn NCQG target.
Context: COP29 set the New Collective Quantified Goal (NCQG),
calling on all actors to scale climate finance for developing countries
to at least $1.3tn a year by 2035. No country has an assigned share,

and private capital is expected to count.
At COP31: first implementation update on the Baku-Belem roadmap
to $1.3T, based on “measurable progress” against targets including:
• a global monitoring framework to track progress in climate finance
mobilization, identify bottlenecks, and highlight emerging solutions.
• clear pathways for public, private, domestic and international
finance;
• Expanding operability, comparability, and interoperability of

climate-finance data; and
• action on grants and concessional finance, debt and fiscal space,
private-finance costs, capacity and system reform.
Are the NCQG and IHLEG aligned? IHLEG’s $1.3tn external-finance
estimate (within the $3.2tn needed excl. China) does align with the
NCQG $1.3tn goal and underpins the Baku-to-Belém Roadmap.
However, they are built differently: political target vs needs-based
estimate.
$300 billion a year by 2035
COP31 focus: what counts and who contributes. This amount

contributes towards the $1.3tn NCQG target from all actors.
Context: Developed countries agreed to lead mobilisation of at least
$300bn a year by 2035, counting public finance and the private
finance it mobilises; no country has a fixed share.
At COP31: Focus turns to how to deliver. Developing countries are
pushing for the development of action plans to chart a pathway to
$300bn and for the Article 9 work programme to be included on the
formal agenda (with emphasis on Article 9.1 – which explicitly covers

developed country finance provision).
Adaptation finance
COP31 focus: embedding and tracking the tripling pledge
Context: COP30 called for adaptation finance to at least triple by
2035, yet this was relatively vague; it did not specify a base year or a
target figure (some have suggested $120bn, i.e. 3x the 2025 goal).
At COP31: Parties need to resolve disagreement on whether to
anchor the pledge in the Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) and put a
finer point on the agreed Belém Adaptation Indicators that will track

it, after disagreement stalled the June Bonn talks.
Loss and Damage Fund
COP31 focus: guidance, replenishment and access
Why it matters: The Fund is the only dedicated channel for
irreversible climate impacts, yet it has no annual funding target and
resources remain far below demand.
At COP31: Focus will be on its long-term funding strategy, with the
first replenishment cycle set to begin in 2027. $342m made available
in the first funding window, which offers $5–20m grants with at least

half reserved for SIDS and LDCs; but the voluntary nature of
payments, disputes over who pays and sensitivity around
“compensation” make major new pledges in Antalya unlikely.

Developing countries want grants (especially fo r

Pressure on development banks
The US is pushing MDBs (e.g. the World Bank), to drop

Donors are diverting aid to fund domestic priorities

The IHLEG estimates ~½ the $1.3tn could come from
private sources. Private capital is looking for regulation,
insurance and more guarantees to reduce risk.

### Page 8

TO P I C  3 | AD AP TATI O N  &  R ES I L I EN C E (1  o f 2 )
Adaptation will be one of the most important, and more divisive, negotiating topics
Adaptation is also set to be on the formal negotiating agenda at COP31. UN warnings that an overshoot of the Paris Agreement’s 1.5°C goal is now unavoidable (at least temporarily), have
dampened morale for climate activists. As it relates to the COP agenda on adaptation, parties appear deadlocked on whether the COP30 goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035 should be

included in the Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) text. This is important, as anchoring it to the GGA could signal to investors the scale and timing of future public and blended finance flows.
The role of adaptation in the COP process
The Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) was established under the Paris Agreement to enhance
adaptive capacity, strengthen resilience and reduce vulnerability to climate change. At COP30
last year, countries adopted 59 indicators (still being refined) to measure progress against the

goal. They also agreed a goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035. The pledge was vague: it set
neither a concrete figure nor a baseline year (a potential annual target of $120bn would be
triple the previous goal of c.$40bn by 2025).
Why GGA talks were deadlocked at the June climate meetings
Finance was the main sticking point at the June Bonn meetings. Developing countries wanted
the COP30 goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035 referenced in the GGA text; countries

including Canada, Norway and Japan opposed. Parties also split on whether the indicators task
force should be expert- or party-led, so no GGA text was agreed and the decision passes to
COP31. Moving the Adaptation Fund to serve the Paris Agreement exclusively (funded by a 5%
share of proceeds from Article 6.4 credits) stalled in Bonn over the composition of its board;
talks will resume at COP31.
What is needed at COP31 to unlock adaptation progress

Adaptation is on the provisional COP31 agenda, including the tripling adaptation finance goal
(though this may get blocked). Some parties want it explicitly referenced in the final COP31
GGA decision; this should become clearer when the agenda is adopted as COP31 opens on 9
Nov. At issue: the tripling pledge still lacks a baseline and named contributors. At COP30,
developed nations resisted new finance obligations, while the EU and many others pushed for

a roadmap to transition away from fossil fuels, which a group of mostly oil- and gas-producing
countries blocked. The result was a looser tripling goal and no agreed fossil-fuel roadmap.
Developing countries are unlikely to give ground, as adaptation relies far more on public
funding than mitigation, so a repeat of COP30’s finance vs. mitigation debate is plausible.
Sources: United Nations, UNEP, Carbon Brief, European Parliament (COP30 outcomes briefing, December 2025)

### Page 9

TO P I C  3 | AD AP TATI O N  &  R ES I L I EN C E (2  o f 2 )
The EU’s proposed Climate Resilience Law plans for a 3°C world, while reiterating commitment to net zero 2050
The EU Commission’s recently proposed Climate Resilience Law would set a reference global warming trajectory of 3°C, providing a benchmark for member states’ adaptation planning. The EU
states it remains committed to reaching net zero by 2050, but some at COP will likely view the proposal as accepting overshoot. With the EU also pointing to an estimated need for EUR70bn in

annual adaptation investment, this provides an interesting backdrop as COP31 seeks to resume adaptation negotiations which stalled earlier this year, primarily due to finance.
What the Commission proposed
On 27 October the Commission presented its EU Framework for Climate Resilience, anchored
by a proposed European Climate Resilience Law. With current global policy commitments
pointing to around 2°C of warming by 2050 and 3°C by 2100, the law would set a common

reference trajectory of 1.5°C in 2030, 2°C in 2050 and 3°C in 2100, with this regarded as the
“baseline assumption”. Member states would be required to use it in national climate risk
assessments and adopt a single national resilience plan by end-2030.
Why it matters: the EU is planning for overshoot
The EU highlighted warnings that overshoot of the 1.5°C goal is now “unavoidable” (even if just
temporarily) as necessitating policy action, even as the EU says it is still committed to climate

neutrality by 2050. The Commission links the plan to Europe’s hottest summer on record and
estimates annual economic losses from climate extremes of €50bn over the past four years,
adding that output losses caused by climate change go far beyond this. It says EU adaptation
needs are ~€70bn a year through to 2050, with Europe warming at 2x the global rate.
What it means for COP31
The proposal demonstrates that warnings on the implications of a significant overshoot of the

Paris Agreement global warming limits are moving from scientific theory to real government
policy. While the opening line of the new EU framework emphasized it remains committed to
reaching net zero by 2050, some may view a 3°C planning baseline as accepting overshoot.
The headline €70bn annual EU adaptation finance requirement also provides an interesting
backdrop heading into Global Goal on Adaptation talks given they are stalled largely due to

disagreement over explicit links to finance in the GGA text. For context, the UNEP estimates
annual adaptation needs for developing countries of $310–365bn by 2035. Ultimately, we
expect adaptation focus to continue growing as warming impacts are increasingly felt and
societal calls for relief grow louder. Corporates will need to consider potential strategic
impacts, while some investors have already begun raising their baseline warming forecasts.

### Page 10

TO P I C  4 | EL EC TR I F I C ATI O N
Türkiye is championing a voluntary pledge to lift electrification from 23% to 35% by 2035
Electrification is expected to be at the centre of this “implementation COP”, targeting a rise in electricity’s share of global final energy use from 23% to 35% by 2035. We expect support for this
action given it is being positioned alongside energy security, but we also expect discussion and debate over how it is delivered (e.g., the role of renewables, nuclear and natural gas), alongside

concerns over clean tech dependency in supply chains. China and the EU have expressed support but not yet committed.
Some may brand this the electrification COP
The Action Agenda pledge aims to raise electricity's share of global final energy from 23%
today to 35% by 2035. The pledge was launched in Bonn this past June and formalised as the
"35-by-35 Global Electrification Pledge" in New York on 21 September.
China and the EU have signaled support, but have not yet signed the pledge

Electrification fits modern day energy-security goals making it a viable target for this year’s
COP. China’s cleantech dominance means it has a further incentive to support initiatives that
seek to increase the pace of global electrification. It also aligns closely with China's existing
development trajectory. While the 35% goal is based on Paris-aligned pathways supported by
IEA and IRENA analysis, the numerical target itself does not require the additional electricity to

come from non fossil fuel sources. This has invited some criticism, though it may make it
easier for some nations to sign the non-binding pledge.
Alignment with the voluntary renewables pledge
Recall that COP28 called for tripling global renewable power capacity by 2030 in COP28’s
formal Global Stocktake decision, equivalent to around 11.2 TW. IRENA’s September 2026
assessment puts capacity at 5.15 TW at end-2025. Record additions of 693 GW in 2025 remain

below the 1,200 GW needed annually in 2026–30. The 35-by-35 pledge complements this
supply goal by shifting transport, buildings and industry towards electricity, enabling
renewable power to replace fossil fuels. Its emissions benefit still depends on cleaner
generation.
We view the goal positively, but delivery needs grid enhancements.
IRENA counts ~2,500 GW of renewable, storage and large-load projects awaiting connection
and says annual investment to strengthen grid infrastructure and enhance system flexibility

must rise from ~$500bn in 2025 to around $1tn through 2035, and $1.2tn thereafter. That
said, signatories signal support, but domestic delivery plans will determine investment.
Sources: UNFCCC, COP31 Presidency, IEA, IRENA
10
World
35% goal

TO P I C  5   |   F O S S I L  F U EL S
The fossil fuel transition roadmap track is growing outside the formal COP process
COP30 failed to agree a fossil fuel transition roadmap, despite support from more than 80 countries. Brazil’s COP30 presidency is therefore developing a roadmap outside formal UN
negotiations to be presented ahead of COP31. Separately, Colombia and the Netherlands convened a ‘coalition of the willing’ of 57 countries in Santa Marta in April 2026 to explore practical

steps towards a fossil fuel phase-out. This parallel forum allows willing countries to cooperate without needing consensus from all COP parties.
Sources: Carbon Brief, Santa Marta conference report

TO P I C  6 | TR AD E
Trade could resurface at COP31 as a challenge to collective agreement; talks have been requested
Trade tensions could feature at COP31 as governments weigh climate ambition against industrial competitiveness. Developing countries fear that measures such as the CBAM disadvantage

their exporters, while the EU says they prevent carbon leakage. COP30 agreed three annual dialogues on trade's role in climate cooperation (no binding outcome), creating for the first time a
formal space for trade discussions in the UNFCCC process, but The African Group and others would like it on the formal COP31 agenda.
Do trade discussions belong at COP?
Developing countries have long pushed back against climate-related trade measures such as
the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) and Deforestation Regulation (EUDR).

They argue such measures are protectionism disguised as climate policy and
disproportionately impact poorer countries. Developed countries view such measures as
necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases where foreign competitors face
lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic industries, while also arguing
that COP is not the appropriate forum to discuss trade matters. Despite this, a European
Parliament briefing noted trade is a point of “exposure” for the EU at COP31.

COP30’s agreement on trade dialogue was notable, but it may not be enough
COP30 agreed three annual climate-trade dialogues at the mid-year sessions in 2026-28, with
a high-level event in 2028. The first, in Bonn (13 June), was calmer than expected but exposed
the divide: developing countries argued that climate-linked trade measures such as CBAM
raise costs and restrict market access and called for impact assessments within the UNFCCC,
while the EU defended them as responses to environmental externalities. With trade also

raised in other Bonn rooms, it is far from clear the dialogue will contain the issue in Antalya.
The African Group of negotiators has requested a Trade dialogue on the formal agenda.
China has already said it intends to use COP31 to oppose cleantech trade barriers, with its vice
minister of the Ministry of Ecology and Environment saying the event should discuss “in depth”
the “new problem of unilateralism and protectionism undermining global collective efforts” on

climate. The African Group is also pushing for trade to be on the formal agenda. Ultimately,
trade talks could prove more consequential at COP31 than in previous years given inflationary
pressures and concerns over business competitiveness, but it is hard to assess the potential
impact other than possibly causing delays in agreement. However, even if the impact is
limited, the issue could shape negotiations elsewhere outside of the COP process.
Sources: European Parliament, Carbon Brief, Reuters

### Page 13

TOP I C 7 | G EO P O L I TI CS  & MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (1  o f 2 )
Multilateralism is at a crossroads
Geopolitical fragmentation and the US exit from the Paris Agreement make consensus among nearly 200 nations harder, encouraging “coalitions of the willing” (outside of the formal COP
negotiations) to pursue more ambitious climate goals. COP31’s success or failure in securing multilateral agreement or credible voluntary pledges could shape future COPs by showing which

approaches deliver progress. The US retreat from the Paris Agreement will also continue to have far-reaching implications for climate finance. Overall, the EU and China will be expected to lead.
The consensus agreement principle under strain
The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) provides the
multilateral framework for COP, the forum where countries negotiate collective climate
decisions. The process is anchored by the view that all countries must act on climate change,

but the extent of their efforts depends on national circumstances. This puts the onus on
developed countries to lead. The question is whether this principle still holds against a more
fragmented backdrop and transactional diplomatic context as national budgets prioritize
security and business competitiveness. With the COP process still separating developed and
developing countries by their 1992 UNFCCC classifications, developed countries are continuing

to push for wealthier developing nations such as China and Saudi Arabia to play a greater role
on finance. This has been a major point of contention at recent summits.
The US stepping back has far-reaching implications
The US skipped COP30 and left the Paris Agreement. It will formally exit the UNFCCC in
February 2027. This retreat has weighed on broader climate-finance decisions, both from a
monetary sense and through the loss of negotiating power. While cities, states and businesses

from the US continue to support COP efforts (e.g., groups like America Is All In), the federal
administration’s absence has put more pressure on the EU and China to lead.
What does an evolving process look like?
Because formal COP decisions require consensus (or lack of objection), progress often stalls or
moves slowly. COP30 saw Brazil move items where there was strong, but not unanimous,
support such as the fossil fuel roadmap, to separate streams outside the formal COP process.

Countries disappointed with the lack of stronger mitigation action also launched a separate
summit on transitioning away from fossil fuels, held this past April in Colombia. While COP is
the flagship global forum, these actions demonstrate a shift towards “coalitions of the willing”
for issues where global agreement is not possible.
Sources: EU Council, EU Parliament, Carbon Brief, UN, WRI

TO P I C  7 | G EO P O L I TI C S  &  MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (2  o f 2 )
Much will rest on the approach of the EU and China

With the US absent, the EU and China are the two actors with the greatest diplomatic and economic leverage with which to broker compromise agreements between the different factions at
COP31. This will require careful diplomacy, with their interests not fully aligned in some areas; discussions around trade and climate finance may be particularly challenging. At the same time,
both have reiterated their continued support of climate action and multilateral decision making; a successful COP outcome would back up these commitments.

### Page 15

TO P I C  8 | AI  &  C L I MATE
The Antalya AI Pledge puts data-centre power demand on the agenda
We expect AI’s climate potential and rising electricity demand to make it a prominent COP31 topic, prompting debate over COPs’ role in data-centre energy use. The Antalya AI Pledge invites
governments and companies to commit voluntarily to sustainable infrastructure and environmental transparency. Its call for zero- and low-emissions energy infrastructure leaves room for

nuclear power, prompting discussion of its role in meeting data-centre demand alongside renewables.
The Antalya AI Pledge seeks to build stakeholder support for sustainable AI buildout
Türkiye is encouraging stakeholders (inc. corporates) to commit to the pledge, asking
signatories to “declare our shared ambition to make AI part of the climate solution. This
means sustainably powered, efficiently designed, transparently measured and available to all”.

Signatories will commit to: 1) Power and build AI infrastructure and hardware sustainably, 2)
Design, manage or procure AI responsibly across its lifecycle, 3) Support assessment and
reporting of the environmental impacts of AI systems, 4) Strengthen financial support and
technical infrastructure for AI for climate action.
The AI for Clean Technologies Initiative seeks to highlight AI climate solutions
The initiative plans to develop AI-enabled pilot projects (e.g. smart energy systems) and then

link the projects with industry partners and investors, aiming to demonstrate AI’s potential to
contribute to sustainability goals. For grids, the IEA sees AI improving forecasting, congestion
management and outage response, helping operators get more from existing lines.
The dialogue here will set the stage for AI discussions at future COPs
While the pledge is voluntary, many of these themes are already beginning to appear in early-
stage regulatory demands, with transparency and disclosure receiving particular focus. There

will be questions over how effective the pledge can be given the US has disengaged from the
COP process, though California could send a strong signal if it commits to the pledge. We will
be particularly focused on how the tech industry responds. Despite the trend of greenhushing,
we expect many to send delegations to Antalya, with AI companies and investors viewing
responsible practices and community engagement as key to avoiding backlash and delays to

AI buildout. Note that responsible AI was also a hot topic at the Principles for Responsible
Investment conference this year in October.
Sources: COP31 Presidency, IEA

### Page 16

TO P I C  9 | N ATU R E &  C AR B O N  MAR K ETS
Nature and carbon markets have key roles to play in supporting climate action
Carbon credit discussions will centre on government policies to boost demand and corporate engagement, with much of the formal UN architecture now in place. If carbon markets can scale,
the benefits would be wide-ranging (e.g. supporting emissions targets and providing finance for developing countries). Credibility and greenwashing concerns remain key risks however; we

expect prices to diverge in favour of high-integrity credits. The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF) now has ~$7.8bn of sponsor capital against a $10bn end-2026 target.
Sources: Tropical Forest Forever Facility, Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
16
Voluntary carbon market development
Türkiye has stated a focus on carbon markets at COP31, particularly on government actions to
boost demand, highlighting the potential for carbon markets to unlock more than $50bn in

additional annual finance for developing nations. The Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
(made up of 13 governments) has announced it will unveil a “Policy Playbook” at COP31 to
support market development. While much of Article 6 (carbon credits under UN process) has
moved to delivery, countries will aim to reach agreement on the funding model for Article 6.2
at COP31. Further carbon market progress stands to have multiple benefits; it can support
developed countries in hitting climate goals while reducing immediate decarbonisation

requirements, while also providing finance and supporting ecosystems in developing ones.
Understanding the pushback
Carbon markets have seen pushback over credit integrity (including regulatory scrutiny) and
the risk that offsets delay domestic cuts. UN involvement is needed to enforce common
accounting and integrity rules, such as the Article 6 rulebook finalised at COP29 and due for
review in 2028, that stop the same reduction being claimed twice. Without that backstop,

fragmented bilateral and voluntary schemes risk eroding the trust needed to scale the market.
Update on the TFFF
The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30 last year, aims for $10bn of
sponsor contributions by year-end. It pays tropical-forest countries per hectare conserved,
discounts payments for each hectare lost or degraded, and directs at least 20% to Indigenous
Peoples and local communities. Its Charter was adopted on 22 July 2026 and received a

tailwind following the re-election of Brazilian President Lula, who is a key supporter. Shortly
after Lula’s re-election, the Netherlands pledged up to $500mn to the fund, bringing the total
pledged to $7.8bn. Further financing commitments are key, with Norway’s $3bn contribution
conditional on the fund reaching at least $10bn by the end of 2026.
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026

TO P I C  1 0 | P AC I F I C , O C EANS  & S EA L EV EL  R I S E
Oceans will be a priority item, with Australia promising to champion Pacific interests
Australia’s ask for ceding the hosting was a Pacific pre-COP, and it has promised to continue championing Pacific interests at COP31. The Taku Pakasoa Declaration, signed at the pre-COP,

reflects Pacific priorities, calling for faster emissions cuts, more adaptation finance and financial system reform. The climate-ocean nexus will also be a focus, including investable solutions.
Championing Pacific interests is a key focus for Australia
As part of the hosting compromise, Australia co-hosted the official pre-COP with Fiji and
Tuvalu (5–8 Oct), attended by countries including Germany, the UAE and India (though some
questioned the low number of international leaders who attended). Australia has pledged to

keep championing Pacific interests as President of the Negotiations.
Taku Pakasoa Declaration reflects Pacific Priorities
Signed on 6 Oct at the pre-COP, it calls for faster emissions cuts, a substantial increase in
climate finance (particularly for adaptation in capacity-constrained countries), reform of the
international financial architecture, and for elevating the ocean-climate nexus. It also
welcomes the 35% electrification goal.
The challenges of rising sea levels are gaining increasing prominence

Sea levels in the region are rising faster than the global average, and half of Pacific Island
infrastructure lies within 500 metres of the coast. The UN General Assembly adopted the UN
Declaration on Sea Level Rise by consensus on 24 Sept; global sea levels rose nearly 6mm in
2024, the highest annual increase on record.
Oceans are key to climate stability, but more finance is needed
Pacific nations emphasise the critical role of oceans as a natural environmental regulator.

They absorb 30% of all carbon dioxide emissions (largest carbon sink) and capture 90% of the
excess heat generated by these emissions. Investor interest in sustainable ocean solutions is
growing, with 2026 YTD blue bond issuance already an annual record, yet bond issuance
supporting UN SDG 14: Life Below Water trails many other categories; scaling this up will be a
focus at COP31. A commitment to delivering “tangible, bankable ocean outcomes” at COP31 is

one of 13 points included as part of the Trabzon Ocean Call to Action unveiled by Türkiye,
while the ORRAA has unveiled a Blue Climate Finance Pathway.
Sources: Taku Pakasoa Declaration (UNFCCC), UN Declaration on Sea Level Rise

F U TU R E O F  C O P
Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, with China reportedly considering a bid and South Korea potentially interested. COP31–33 will test delivery in a fragmented world, with a second

Global Stocktake concluding in 2028. Its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

### Page 19

AP P END I X  |   COP  TER MS
Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
Sources: UNFCCC; COP30 and COP31 Presidency documents; and the other sources referenced in this report
19
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
TERM DEFINITION

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework for international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of carbon credits between countries.
Article 9

Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies (e.g. SB64) in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.
Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.

Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.
COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum is COP President and leads the Action Agenda.
TERM DEFINITION
Final COP text Decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome.

Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.
Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the link to finance is contested.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the

shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.
Negotiating groups Blocs that coordinate national positions, such as the G77 & China, the EU, LDCs and AOSIS.

Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
Pre-COP Ministerial meeting ahead of the COP to test positions and build consensus. For COP31, Australia co-hosted a Pacific pre-COP.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafts final texts and steers government-to-government talks.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

Appendix C: The level of alarm over global warming has begun to move higher (again)
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

## Document 385: NZ labour market review chartpack, Q3 2026 (2026-11-06)

Source PDF: all report pdf/NZ labour market review chartpack, Q3 2026 (2026-11-06).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-06

### Page 1

LABOUR MARKET CHARTPACK
4 Nov 2026

### Page 2

Westpac New Zealand Economics
2
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us
Key points – September quarter 2026
• The September quarter surveys pointed to ongoing softness in labour market conditions. The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing an
almost five-year run of increases. The unemployment rate is now at its highest level since September 2015.
• There were mixed messages in terms of jobs growth: the Household Labour Force Survey reported a 0.1% fall in employment, while the employer-

focused Quarterly Employment Survey suggested a modest rise. The Monthly Employment Indicator had also pointed to a small rise in jobs.
• The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in the participation rate. These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly large
increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error in the survey rather than a
genuine surge.
• We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate, which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message here is one of softness. Our

best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the growth in the population.
• While the economy has returned to growth over the last year or so, hiring activity remains weak. This may be an ongoing legacy of employment trends in
previous years, when firms were relatively reluctant to lay people off during the slowdown, which then potentially left them overstaffed even as activity
started to pick up again. It’s not clear how long this effect can persist, though.

• Wage growth remains muted. Although cost-of-living pressures have increased on workers, the existing degree of spare capacity in the labour market
doesn’t give them much leverage to negotiate larger pay increases.
• Overall, we judge these results to be weaker than the RBNZ expected, in particular the rise in the unemployment rate. This data supports the views of
those on the policy committee who were less convinced in October about the potential for second-round inflation pressures in the economy.

### Page 3

Westpac New Zealand Economics
3
Employment
• The number of people employed fell by 0.1% in the September quarter, according to the
Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS). Previous quarters were revised down a little,
reflecting downward revisions to the working-age population estimates. Annual employment
growth slowed from 0.9% to 0.7%.
• This was substantially softer than our forecast of a 0.5% rise for the quarter. Our forecast
was based on the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI), a comprehensive measure that is

drawn from income tax data. While there are some differences in coverage for these two
measures, any significant divergence between the two is most likely to reflect sampling error
in the HLFS.
• Meanwhile, the employer-focused Quarterly Employment Survey (QES) recorded a 0.1% rise
in the number of filled jobs and a 0.6% rise in hours paid. (This survey is of course vulnerable
to sampling error as well.)
• We’re most inclined towards the MEI measure of employment, given the breadth of its

coverage. But our best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not
yet fast enough to absorb the growth in the adult population – hence the further rise in the
unemployment rate.

### Page 4

Westpac New Zealand Economics
4
Labour force participation
• The surprising fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a drop in the participation rate
from 70.6% to 70.4%.
• These co-movements are effectively a reversal of the surprisingly large increases that we
saw in the June quarter, which we noted at the time were suggestive of sampling error in the
survey. Any such errors largely wash out when it comes to the unemployment rate, however,
which is why we recommend focusing on this measure.

• Setting aside the usual quarterly volatility, labour force participation remains caught
between two competing forces. One is cyclical weakness, especially among younger people,
who are finding it difficult to break into a “low-fire, low-hire” labour market. The teen
unemployment rate of around 25% is a record high going back to the start of the survey
in 1986.
• The other force is rising participation among the over-60s (who also make up a growing

share of the population). Kiwis have had a growing propensity to work beyond the NZ Super
age, as there is no penalty for doing so. It’s unclear to what extent this can be considered
an increase in the pool of available workers: older workers tend to have low mobility
between jobs, and if they find themselves out of work are more likely to leave the labour
force altogether.

### Page 5

Westpac New Zealand Economics
5
Unemployment and underutilisation
• The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7% in the September quarter. This was the
highest rate since September 2015.
• While there are other indicators of spare capacity in the labour market, we’re not convinced
that there is additional information to draw from them this time.
• The broader underutilisation measure, which includes discouraged jobseekers and part-
time workers wanting more hours, was steady at 13.8% after having risen sharply from

12.9% last quarter. This measure is both higher (by definition) and more variable than the
unemployment rate, but tells a similar story in that it’s around an 11-year high.
• There was a marked 1.6% fall in the number of hours worked (although ‘usual’ hours were
down just 0.4%). However, we suspect this was largely driven by Matariki – a floating holiday
that won’t be captured in the seasonal adjustment.
• This may also account for the 2.5% rise in part-time employment, against a 0.7% fall in

full-time employment. Some workers may have been pushed into the part-time category
(less than 30 hours a week) simply by virtue of only working four days during the
survey week.

### Page 6

Westpac New Zealand Economics
6
Hiring
• Business confidence surveys have shown a lift in hiring intentions in recent months, as firms
have shaken off the initial shock of the Iran conflict and the surge in fuel prices.
• However, those stated intentions aren’t obviously turning into reality yet. Job advertisements
were down about 1% for the September quarter in seasonally adjusted terms, and they
remain well below pre-Covid levels. Job ads will capture ‘churn’ within existing roles as well

as new job creation, but both of these would be associated with a strong labour market.
• With the working-age population growing at the same time, the result was a modest fall in
the vacancy rate, one of the RBNZ’s highest-ranked measures of labour market tightness.
• We estimate that there are around 1.9 people unemployed for every job vacancy, up from
around 1.8 people this time last year.
• Firms have told us that they were reluctant to lay off workers during the economic downturn

in 2023-24, as they were wary of the extreme labour shortages that had developed in the
previous boom years. As a result, they may find themselves still overstaffed even as demand
has started to pick up again, and haven’t needed to return to hiring yet.

### Page 7

Westpac New Zealand Economics
7
Population and migration
• The working-age population grew by around 1.0% over the last year, compared to a peak of
3.3% growth over 2023. Jobs growth (based on the less volatile MEI measure) is still lagging
behind population growth on an annual basis.
• The pool of potential workers is generally becoming older, with the Baby Boomers
increasingly reaching pension age and beyond, and with migrant arrivals tending to be an

older cohort than we saw pre-Covid – more in their 30s and 40s than their 20s.
• Net migration flows have started to pick up from their lows since late 2025. Departures of
New Zealanders have come down from their highs, though they remain above pre-Covid
levels, as the relative appeal of the Australian jobs market has narrowed.
• Foreign arrivals have picked up to some degree, although the growth appears to have been
mostly driven by students rather than those on work visas.

### Page 8

Westpac New Zealand Economics
8
Sectoral and regional trends
• Jobs growth has been relatively strong in government-connected sectors such as healthcare
and education, as well as in local and central government administration.
• Mining and utilities have seen strong growth in percentage terms, but neither of these
sectors are big employers in absolute terms.
• The construction sector is now starting to see a lift in employment, after a steep decline

over 2024-25. Residential building consents have risen strongly over the last year or so,
pointing to a growing pipeline of work for the homebuilding industry.
• Sectors that are connected to international tourism, such as transport and recreational
services, had previously seen strong employment growth, but this appears to have flattened
out in recent months.
• Employment trends have generally been more positive in the South Island than the North,

which likely reflects the greater influence of the agricultural sector – while not a large
employer in itself, farmers have benefited from high export prices and their spending has
flowed into other sectors.

### Page 9

Westpac New Zealand Economics
9
Wages
• The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September quarter. Annual growth
remained around the lowest since 2021.
• While the overall result was in line with our forecast, the details were a little weaker than
we expected, with less contribution from private sector wages (up 0.5%) and a surprisingly
large 0.7% increase in the public sector.
• The distribution of pay rates has returned to pre-Covid trends. Only 44% of roles saw a

pay rise over the last year; of those roles that did see an increase, the average size has
converged on the 2-3% range.
• The LCI is not a measure of what workers actually receive – it reflects the cost to businesses
for a given unit of labour – and hence shouldn’t be compared to the inflation rate. A more
suitable measure is the analytical unadjusted LCI, which includes pay rises that are related
to productivity gains. This measure slowed from 3.1% to 2.9% on an annual basis, the lowest

since March 2021.
• There was a marked pickup in the proportion of firms reporting cost-of-living adjustments as
the main reason for pay increases. However, the size of those increases varied greatly, with
many of them below the rate of CPI inflation.
• This seems to reflect a shift in the justification for pay rises, rather than an overall increase in
wage pressures – firms are more likely to cite cost-of-living adjustments because they’re less
likely to cite demand-side pressures.

### Page 10

Westpac New Zealand Economics
10
International comparisons
• New Zealand’s unemployment rate is the 18th lowest in the OECD (out of 38 countries). This
ranking has been broadly unchanged over the last year or so.
• Notably, New Zealand’s unemployment rate is substantially above Australia’s, whereas
historically it has been lower on average. This shift in relative job opportunities has been a
key attractor for New Zealanders over the last couple of years – as it was in the early 2010s

when the Australian economy was benefiting from a boom in mining construction.
• However, that gap between unemployment rates is no longer widening, with the Australian
jobs market starting to soften this year as the RBA has raised interest rates to rein in inflation
(which was already running too high before the Iran conflict and the surge in oil prices). We
expect Australia’s unemployment to rise further over the year ahead.
• New Zealand has the 7th highest employment rate in the OECD (down from 6th last quarter).

We tend to have high rates of labour force participation for 15-64 year olds, and particularly
high participation for the over-65s, as the lack of means testing for NZ Superannuation
means there is no financial disincentive to working beyond pension age.

The scenario, forecasts and nowcasts of the Economic Research 
– 
2
8 
September 
2026

### Page 2

UNITED STATES 
The US economy is expected to grow above its potential in 2026, with an average annual growth rate of 2. 2%, close to the 2025 rate (2.1%) . 
This apparent resilience to the uncertainty, tariff and energy shocks, together with above-trend productivity growth, masks K-shaped growth, 
driven by investment linked to AI-optimism and consumption by the wealthiest amid a wealth effect (historically high stock market valuations). 
Inflation overshooting is set to continue (3.4% in 2026) at least through 2028, largely because of the rise in oil prices and tariffs – although the 
impact of these latter appears to be less significant than expected. The labour market is showing clear signs of improvement that should bring 
the unemployment rate down toward 4.0%. Given this shift in price and employment risks amid dynamic growth, the FOMC proceeded with a 
first rate hike at September’s meeting and we expect two further 25bp increases in December 2026 and January 2027, bringing the Fed Funds 
target range at 4,25% - 4,50%.

CHINA 
Economic growth slowed to 4.3% y/y in Q2 2026 vs. 5.0% in Q1. It is projected to reach 4.6% in 2026 as a whole, down from 5% in 2025. Growth 
remains characterized by a K-shaped trajectory, and the gap between the strong performance of exports and the fragility of sectors that rely 
on domestic demand  has even widened this year . Domestic demand has weakened significantly in recent months . Retail sales have barely 
increased, the crisis in the property sector has continued and household confidence remains low. Fiscal policy measures aimed at supporting 
economic growth are expected to increase in the second half of the year. CPI inflation has rebounded slightly since last March, mostly due to 
the rise in global energy prices, as well as due to the increase in electronic good prices and anti-involution measures implemented by the 
authorities. Deflationary pressures persist, however, given weakness in domestic demand.

EUROZONE 
Eurozone growth is expected to slow to 1.1% in 2026 (from 1.3% in 2025), before strengthening to 1.5% in 2027, supported by higher defence 
and infrastructure spending as well as the global AI capex cycle. The economy has proven resilient to the energy shock, notab ly thanks to the 
increasingly tangible impact of G erman defence spending on the real economy. Elevated energy prices in the autumn (especially for natural 
gas), combined with resilient growth, are set to accelerate inflation to a peak around the turn of the year. Annual average i nflation is thus 
expected to reach  3.0% in 2026 (from 2.1% in 2025), before easing to 2.8% in 2027. Given persistent inflation and resilient growth, we 
anticipate another 25bp hike from the ECB in December 2026, bringing the deposit rate to 2.75%, followed by an extended pause . That said, 
stronger than expected second-round effects could lead to more tightening than our current baseline.

ECONOMIC SCENARIO AND FORECAST

Contacts 
Stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com  
Tarik.rharrab@bnpparibas.com

### Page 3

FRANCE 
GDP growth remained low in Q2 (0% after -0.2% in Q1) as a result of deteriorated agricultural and construction output, as well as higher 
inflation (2.4% in 2026 according to our forecast, after 0.9% in 2025) . In 2026, GDP growth should  reach 0.5% (after 0.9% in 2025), driven 
mainly by exports.

UNITED KINGDOM 
UK GDP growth would ease slightly to 1.2% in 2026 (1.3% in 2025), before edging back to 1.3% in 2027. The economy has so far weathered 
both the energy price shock and domestic political volatility. That said, growth is expected to decelerate in the second half  of the year, while 
inflation is set to remain stubbornly above target (averaging 3.2% in both 2026 and 2027), driven by higher energy prices and mounting second-
round effects. In response, we expect the BoE to deliver a single insurance hike in November 2026, bringing the Bank Rate to 4.00%. The BoE 
is then expected to resume easing in H2 2027, with two cuts taking the Bank Rate to 3.50% by end-year.

JAPAN 
Following 1.2% in 2025, Japan's GDP growth is expected to decelerate to 1.0% in 2026, before rebounding to 1.2% in 2027. Higher inflation 
and production costs associated with the energy shock are expected to weigh on the economy’s overall performance. On the other hand, the 
latter is supported by fiscal policy  and AI-related investment. Inflation has generally overshot the 2% y/y target since 2022. Accordingly, the 
Bank of Japan initiated a cautious process of “adjustment in the degree of monetary accommodation” in 2024, lifting the policy rate to 1.25% 
so far (previously negative) – the highest since 1995. We expect two more hikes by the end of Q1 2027, followed by a return to a more cautious 
pace until the terminal rate of +2.5% is reached in H2 2028. Japan is facing long-term rates pressure, illustrated by historically high 10- and 30-
year yields, probably fueled by the level of public debt and the pace of monetary adjustment.

EXCHANGE RATES 
We expect Fed hikes to temporarily offset structural headwinds and support the dollar. EUR/USD would target 1.14 by end -2026, before the 
dollar's structural downtrend resumes toward 1.20 by end-2027, although political and fiscal risk may weigh on the euro next year. The USD/JPY 
appreciation would continue but at a more moderate pace, with the BoJ still behind the curve despite its hikes. By contrast, GBP/USD would 
see relative support underpinned by a modest fiscal risk premium under the new Labour government, and resilient growth.

台燿 6274 TT ( 電子–電子零組件 )

逆風燿進，屹立不搖 
投資與評價 
1. 台燿 3Q26 在泰國廠進度提前開始營收貢獻下，營運創歷史新高；4Q26 預
期將進行 CCL 產品價格調漲，營運將續創歷史新高。 
2. 台燿 26 年泰國廠區布局順利開出，受益於漲價策略帶動營運將逐季增成
長，27 年有望持續提升高階應用佔比，同時持續擴充產能供應，中長期營
運持續成長，預估 26 年 EPS 41.93 元、27 年 EPS 86.20 元。 
3. 考量台燿受惠高階 AI 應用帶動 CCL 規格持續升級，新產能將持續開出，
26、27 年獲利將持續大幅走升 ，調升 27 年獲利預估，目標價上修至 1,900
元(22x 修正後 27(F)PER)，並將投資評等由買進轉為強力買進。

重點更新 
台燿 3Q26 泰國廠進度提前且轉盈，營運創歷史新高 
台燿 3Q26 受益於泰國廠 1-2 期因學習曲線提升，提前一個月至 7M26 開始貢
獻，爬坡期順利，並且持續開出產能貢獻之下，泰國廠順利轉虧為盈且幅度已超
上半年所有虧損，加上來自美系網通交換器、ASIC 專案持續提升貢獻，帶動台
燿 3Q26 營收季增 25.5%達 179.49 億元，優於原估之 173.16 億元(+21.1% 
QoQ)3.7%。毛利率方面，泰國廠因產能爬坡期順利進行，營運轉虧為盈，帶動
毛利率大幅提升 7.2 個百分點至 36.9%優於原預估 33.3%之 3.6 個百分點，且
因營收規模擴大，再加上銷售費用控管得宜，營業費用率下滑至 5.9%，優於原
預估 6.2%，帶動營業利益達 55.68 億元(+65.8% QoQ)，稅後淨利季增 64.7%
至 38.57 億元，EPS 12.88 元，營運達歷史新高。 
4Q26 CCL 產品價格持續調漲，預期台燿營運將續創新高 
展望 4Q26，泰國廠在產能順開之下，將再度開出10 萬張 CCL 產能，因應來自
終端的強勁需求；另外，目前上游材料包含玻纖布、銅箔等持續進行產品價格調
整，且在高階材料越發緊缺之下，M7/M8 等級之 CCL 產品將較過去有這較大
幅度的調漲，故上修台燿 3Q26 營收預估 6.4%至 207.53 億元(+15.6% QoQ)，
受益於高階 AI 應用業務佔比持續攀升與漲價挹注，毛利率預期將提升 1.6 個百
分點至 38.5%；且因營收規模擴大，營業費用率隨之下滑至 5.8%，帶動營業淨
利達 67.94 億元(+22.0% QoQ)，隨之上調預估稅後淨利 25.3%至 50.99 億元
(+32.2% QoQ)，EPS 17.03 元，營運將續創新高。

### Page 2

訪談內容 
台燿 3Q26 泰國廠進度提前且轉盈，營運創歷史新高 
台燿 3Q26 受益於泰國廠 1-2 期因學習曲線提升 ，提前一個月至 7M26 開始貢獻，爬坡期順利 ，
並且持續開出產能貢獻之下，泰國廠順利轉虧為盈且幅度已超上半年所有虧損，加上來自美系網
通交換器、ASIC 專案持續提升貢獻，帶動台燿 3Q26 營收季增 25.5%達 179.49 億元，優於原估
之 173.16 億元(+21.1% QoQ)3.7%。 
毛利率方面，受惠泰國廠營運轉虧為盈，帶動毛利率大幅提升 7.2 個百分點至 36.9%優於原預估
33.3%之 3.6 個百分點，且因營收規模擴大， 再加上銷售費用控管得宜 ， 營業費用率下滑至 5.9%，
優於原預估 6.2%，帶動營業利益達 55.68 億元(+65.8% QoQ)，稅後淨利季增 64.7%至 38.57
億元，EPS 12.88 元，營運達歷史新高。

4Q26 CCL 產品價格持續調漲，預期台燿營運將續創新高 
展望 4Q26，泰國廠在產能順開之下 ， 將再度開出10 萬張 CCL 產能 ， 因應來自終端的強勁需求 ；
另外，目前上游材料包含玻纖布、銅箔等持續進行產品價格調整，且在高階材料越發緊缺之下，
M7/M8 等級之 CCL 產品將較過去有這較大幅度的調漲，故上修台燿 3Q26 營收預估 6.4%至
207.53 億元(+15.6% QoQ)，受益於高階 AI 應用業務佔比持續攀升與漲價挹注，毛利率預期將
提升 1.6 個百分點至 38.5%；且因營收規模擴大，營業費用率隨之下滑至 5.8%，帶動營業淨利
達 67.94 億元(+22.0% QoQ)， 隨之上調預估稅後淨利 25.3%至 50.99 億元(+32.2% QoQ)，EPS 
17.03 元，營運將續創新高。

泰國廠區放量貢獻與高階產品提升，預期台燿 26 年營運創新高 
因應眾多 PCB 板廠於泰國廠產能將於 26 年開出，台燿持續擴大於泰國地區之布局。伴隨泰國廠
1-2 期提前至 7M26 上線，4Q26 將進入全面量產階段，專注於衛星地面板、ASIC 伺服器專案
等產品，伴隨產能逐步釋放，26 年底總產能將達 260 萬張，有望進一步帶動台燿於高階 AI 伺服
器、交換器以及衛星相關板材之供應份額。此外，觀察目前上游原料價格走勢持續飆漲，4Q26
仍將持續進行成本轉嫁調漲策略。 
故上修台燿整體營收預估 3.1%至 630.56 億元(+107.8% YoY)。在漲價效益、產品組合優化以及
泰國廠轉營帶動下，調高毛利率預估至 33.9%至(+11.2 ppts YoY)，並且受益於營運規模擴大與
費用控管， 調降營業費用率預估至 6.1%(-2.3 ppts YoY)，營業利益為 175.48 億元(+304.0% YoY)，
帶動稅後淨利達 125.58 億元(+248.6% YoY)，EPS 42.29 元，獲利翻倍成長且營運創新高。

預期台燿 27 年提升更多高階應用占比，營運將再度創高 
展望 27 年，AI 相關需求在更高傳輸速度及訊號低損耗條件下，終端 PCB 規格持續向上靠攏，同
時板層數也持續往 30 甚至 40 層以上研發，帶動整體 CCL 價量齊揚。台燿將受益於 ASIC 客戶
新一代平台的採用，與 AI 伺服器 CPU 板材規格提升至 M8，以及於高階交換器 800G/ 1.6T 新
客戶進展順利下，帶動台燿高階產品組合持續增加。此外，公司亦有望受惠美系衛星供應商 V3
新一代衛星和 800G/ 1.6T 光模塊之採用。來自終端需求遍地開花之下，台燿 27 年亦將持續擴
充產能，預計於中國常熟及泰國各新增 60 萬張，合計120 萬張產能規劃，年底將達 380 萬張總
產能。故上修台燿 27 年營收預估 2.7%至 1,081,29 億元(+71.5% YoY)。 
伴隨台燿持續提升高端應用佔比，再度調高毛利率預估至 40.0%(+6.0 ppts YoY)，且受益於營收
規模持續大幅提升，調低營業費用率至 6.0%(-0.2 ppts YoY)，預期營業利益將達 367.68 億元
(+109.5% YoY)，稅後淨利季增 105.6%至 258.13 億元，EPS 86.20 元，獲利仍將大幅成長且營
運再度創高。

### Page 4

高階產品出貨比重將持續拉高，帶動台燿 26 及 27 年營運續創新高 
台燿 26 年泰國廠區布局順利開出，配合客戶大量終端需求，已於 3Q26 轉虧為盈，加上漲價策
略帶動下營運將季季增，27 年有望持續提升高階應用佔比，如 AI CPU 、ASIC、Switch 等相關
專案，同時持續擴充產能供應，中長期營運持續成長，預期至 27 年獲利仍保持倍增之成長，預
估 26 年 EPS 42.29 元、27 年 EPS 86.20 元。 
目前台燿股價為 1,375 元，約為 15.5x FY27E EPS ，位於近 3 年 PER 參考區間 5~30x 中間偏下
之位置。考量台燿受惠高階 AI 應用帶動 CCL 規格持續升級，且近兩年擴產計畫將順利、穩定開
出貢獻，將有利公司爭取更多淺在訂單，26、27 年獲利將持續大幅走升。近期針對台燿相關 AI
專案之進展，研究部認為下世代平台之規格尚未訂定，公司仍持續與相關客戶密切合作，加上台
燿近兩年擴產計畫迅速且明確，或將有望拿下更多新專案，搶占相關同業份額。故調升 27 年獲
利預估，但考量未來兩季本業成長增速趨緩，將本益比由近上緣之 25 倍略微調低至中間偏上 22
倍，目標價由 1,975 元(25x 修正前 27(F)PER)上修至 1,900 元(22x 修正後 27(F)PER)，並將投資
評等由買進轉為強力買進。

### Page 5

公司簡介 
台燿科技 TUC（6274 TT） 成立於 1974 年，總部位於台灣新竹縣竹北市，初期以光學玻璃製造 
為主要業務 ，1997 年成功轉型跨入銅箔基板 （Copper Clad Laminate, CCL） 與黏合片 （Prepreg） 
之生產領域，2003 年正式在台掛牌上櫃。台燿為全球高階 CCL 材料領導廠之一，核心產品涵蓋 
高頻、高速、低損耗及高耐熱等系列，廣泛應用於AI 伺服器、網通設備、汽車電子、消費性電子 
與高速資料傳輸板材。公司並提供多層壓合代工（Mass Lamination）服務，具備從原材製造到 
加工整合的一站式能力，形成完整的供應鏈垂直整合優勢。3Q26 營收佔比：ELL&SLL(M7/M8) 
38%、VLL&LL(M4/M6) 28%、HDI&N-LL 19%、Others 15%。

台燿 ESG 政策 
台燿科技秉持「誠信經營、創新永續」的企業核心，積極回應全球淨零減碳趨勢。2024 年全面 
導入 ISO 14064-1 溫室氣體盤查制度 ， 完成第三方查證並據此制定短中長期減量目標 ， 以 2021 
年為基準年，規劃至 2025 年減碳 20%、2030 年減碳 42%、2050 年持續每年再降 2%。公
司同 時持續精進節能管理，2024 年推行多項節電專案，節電量達 21 萬度，能源密集度降至 
19.78 (MWh/百萬元營收)，溫室氣體排放總量 108,554 公噸 CO₂e、強度 11.65 。除推動節
能外， 台燿也積極落實廢棄物源頭減量與回收管理，回收率維持 75％ 以上，並推動綠電採購
與碳費應 對策略。公司依 TCFD 架構揭露氣候風險，將氣候議題納入董事會監督範疇，展現與
國際減碳目 標接軌的決心。 台燿推動永續供應鏈管理，強化上游合作夥伴的 ESG 韌性。2024 
年共完成 26 家主要供應商的 環境與人權稽核，合格率 100％，並要求所有供應商簽署社會責
任與環保承諾書，確保不使用衝 突礦產及有害化學物質。公司已通過  RBA 責任商業聯盟稽核
且未有重大缺失，持續推動供應商 進行溫室氣體盤查與減碳培訓，並逐步導入永續採購制度。
為落實環保製造，台燿全廠導入 ISO 14001 環境管理系統與污染防治方案，強化廢水、廢氣與
噪音監控，確保所有放流水與排放皆符 合法規。透過持續改善與外部驗證，台燿建立透明、低碳
且高效率的供應鏈體系，穩固其作為高 階 CCL 材料領導廠的綠色形象。 
在人本管理方面，台燿視員工為企業最重要的資產。公司全面推行 ISO 45001 職業安全衛生管 
理系統，定期舉辦消防演練、健康檢查及安全教育，2024 年無重大職災事件，勞安指標持續改 
善。人權訓練涵蓋新進人員 100％、在職員工 96％，並透過性平與反歧視政策保障同工同酬。 
2024 年非主管職員工薪資平均 NT$951,877，中位數 NT$858,478，較前一年均提升。公司同 
時持續優化福利與人才培育制度，員工人均訓練時數  9.7 小時，女性員工與男性員工訓練時數  
相當。台燿致力打造安全、健康、包容的職場環境，讓員工能在穩定中成長，於工作中實現自我 
價值。 在公司治理層面，台燿以「誠信、透明、問責」為原則，長期維持公司治理評鑑前 6–20
％ 水準。 董事會中獨立董事席次超過半數，並已將 ESG 績效納入高階主管考核機制。公司制
定道德行為 準則與檢舉機制，嚴禁賄賂、貪腐及任何形式的強迫勞動，並推動供應鏈衝突礦產
盡職調查。資 訊安全方面，台燿建置完善的資安防護系統與個資保護管理制度，2024 年未發生
任何資安事件， 員工資安訓練達 691 人次。透過董事會與內控監督機制，台燿持續強化治理透
明度與風險管理 韌性，奠定企業長期穩健經營的基石。 台燿長期深耕在地公益，2024 年投入
社會關懷、教育與環保活動，推動員工志工、節能減碳倡 議及社區回饋計畫。公司定期舉辦淨灘
與捐血活動，並捐助地方教育與弱勢團體，實踐企業公民 責任。未來，台燿將以 「誠信治理、
綠色製造、人本共榮」 為三大支柱，持續朝 2050 淨零排 放目標邁進，推動電子材料產業的永
續創新，致力成為全球客戶信賴的高階 CCL 永續夥伴。

## Document 547: 中国・７-９月 GDP は前年比＋4.5％に加速も、外需依存の構図は変わらず ～供給サイドが成長をけん引する一方、個人消費と不動産は_f9a4f0d8

Source PDF: all report pdf/中国・７-９月 GDP は前年比＋4.5％に加速も、外需依存の構図は変わらず ～供給サイドが成長をけん引する一方、個人消費と不動産は_f9a4f0d8.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 19 日

1/6 
中国・７-９月 GDP は前年比＋4.5％に加速も、外需依存の構図は変わらず 
～供給サイドが成長をけん引する一方、個人消費と不動産は力強さを欠く展開が続く～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ 中国経済は、世界的なＡＩ・半導体需要を背景に輸出や製造業が堅調に推移する一方、不動
産不況や雇用回復の遅れを背景に個人消費や投資は力強さを欠いており、 「強さ」 と 「弱さ」
が混在している。７-９月の実質ＧＤＰ成長率は前年比＋4.5％と前期から加速し、堅調な外
需を追い風に、製造業、鉱業が景気を下支えする動きが確認された。ただし、企業部門では
商品市況上昇によるコスト増が進む一方、家計の節約志向から最終価格への転嫁は難しく、
企業部門のインフレと消費者部門のディスインフレが併存している。 
○ 政府は個人消費喚起策や財政 ・ 金融支援を強化しており、 ９月の小売売上高（前年比＋1.1％）
には持ち直しの兆しがみられた。ただ、その中身をみると、低価格業態やＥＣへの需要シフ
トが進む一方、 自動車や宝飾品、 娯楽関連などの需要は弱く、 家計の節約志向は依然根強い。
不動産市場も大都市の一部で改善の兆しがみられるものの、地方都市では住宅価格の下落が
続き、不動産投資（年初来前年比▲20.6％）は歴史的な低水準にある。一方、設備投資や公
共投資も含む固定資産投資（年初来前年比▲7.2％）には底打ちの兆しもみられる。 
○ 供給面では、 ９月の鉱工業生産が前年比＋5.5％と加速し、外需の旺盛な動きを追い風にした
ハイテク製造業を中心に堅調さが続いている。内需が弱いなか、中国経済は引き続き外需と
供給サイドへの依存度が高く、国内の需給不均衡も続いている。加えて、原材料価格の上昇
を製品価格へ十分転嫁できず企業収益が圧迫されていることや、不動産規制への懸念から中
国本土株は上値の重い展開が続いている。先行きについては、政策効果の及び方によって業
種間の業績格差が一段と広がる可能性には引き続き注意が必要と考えられる。

【外需の堅調さをけん引役に、７-９月ＧＤＰは前年比＋4.5％に伸びが加速している】 
足元の中国経済を巡っては、 世界的なＡＩ （人工知能） ・ 半導体関連需要の旺盛さを追い風に輸出
は堅調な動きをみせている。こうした動きを背景に、ＡＩ関連やＥＶ（電気自動車）など個別企業
は世界的な競争力を高めるなど、ミクロ面での強さがうかがえる。一方で、長引く不動産不況や若
年層を中心とする雇用回復の遅れなどが個人消費をはじめとする内需は力強さを欠いている。 この
ように、マクロ面でみた中国経済は弱さがうかがえるなど、相反する材料が混在している。

### Page 2

2/6 
こうした状況は、 足元の物価動向にも現れている。 イラン情勢の悪化を受けた原油をはじめとす
る商品市況の上昇を反映して、川上段階の企業部門はインフレに直面している。さらに、企業間取
引段階においては価格転嫁の動きが広がりをみせる流れも確認できる。 しかし、 家計部門が節約志
向を強めるなかで、 企業部門にとっては最終製品への価格転嫁のハードルが高い状況が続いている。
その結果、消費者段階の物価は低迷するディスインフレ基調が続いている。このように、足元の中
国経済はインフレとディスインフレが共存する展開となっている（注１）。 
このように、足元の中国経済は「強さ」と「弱さ」が混在する状況にあるなか、７-９月の実質Ｇ
ＤＰ成長率は前年同期比＋4.5％となり、 前期 （同＋4.3％） から加速した （図１）。 前期比の成長率
も＋1.2％と前期（同＋0.9％）から加速
しており、年率換算では＋ 4.9％と試算
されるなど持ち直しの動きが確認され
ている。分野ごとの動きをみると、個人
消費など内需の弱さを反映して第３次
産業（サービス業）は頭打ちする一方、
第１次産業（農林漁業）の生産は堅調に
推移しているほか、 第２次産業 （製造業 ・
鉱業） の生産が持ち直したことが足元の
景気を下支えしている。イラン情勢の悪
化による商品市況の上昇を受けて、 鉱業
部門の生産が活発化しているほか、 輸出の堅調さが製造業の生産を押し上げる展開が続く。 前述の
ように川上段階でのインフレを受けて、 名目成長率 （前年同期比＋6.0％） は２四半期連続で実質成
長率を上回る伸びとなるなど、マクロ的には「デフレ脱却」が進んでいる。

【個人消費に一見持ち直しの兆しも、その内容をみれば依然として厳しい状況が続いている】 
個人消費をはじめとする内需が力強さを欠くなか、国務院は７月、 同国で初めて個人消費に焦点
を当てた 「消費拡大の５ヵ年計画」を公表した。 同計画では、2030 年の小売売上高 （社会消費品小
売総額）の規模を2025 年（50.1 兆元）に比べて約２割（60 兆元前後）引き上げ、その実現へ消費
構造の高度化や家計所得の引き上げを目指すとした。 加えて、 共産党は７月末に開催した中央政治
局会議において、 インフラ関連など財政支出の進捗加速による景気下支えを図る方針を示した。 財
政部・中銀（中国人民銀行） ・国家金融監督管理総局は、８月17 日付で内需促進を目的とする新た
な財政・金融支援策を発表し、中小・零細民間企業や消費者向け融資への利子補給を拡充すること
を明らかにしている。 
こうした一連の個人消費喚起に向けた動きも追い風に、 ９月の小売売上高 （社会消費品小売総額）

注１ 10 月14 日付レポート「足元の中国経済は、物価の二極化と外需依存が一段と鮮明に」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666553.html）

### Page 3

3/6 
は前年同月比＋1.1％となり、前月（同
＋0.4）から加速して６ヵ月ぶりの高い
伸びとなった。 前月比も＋0.35％と前月
（同▲0.16％） から２ヵ月ぶりの拡大に
転じるとともに、 そのペースも３ヵ月ぶ
りの高い伸びとなるなど、 持ち直しの動
きが確認されている（図２） 。地域別で
は、都市部（前年比＋0.8％）の伸びが
加速しているものの、 農村 （同＋2.7％）
に比べて低い伸びにとどまるなど、 依然
として力強さを欠いている。近年のイン
ターネットの爆発的な普及を追い風に、 小売売上全体に占めるインターネットを通じたＥＣ （電子
商取引）の割合は拡大している。９月は大手ＥＣサイトの大型セールはないものの、中秋節商戦や
秋の新商品投入などを追い風に、オンライン販売は年初来前年比＋4.4％と前月（同＋4.3％）から
わずかに伸びが加速しており、小売売上全体を下支えした。店舗別では、コンビニエンスストアや
スーパーは前年を上回る伸びをみせる一方、 専門店や百貨店などは軒並み前年を下回る伸びにとど
まり、家計部門の節約志向の強まりを受けて、より低価格な業態への需要シフトが進んでいる。 
財別では、不動産に対する需要低迷が続くなかで建材（前年比▲9.2％）や家具（同▲3.0％）の
需要は引き続き低迷している。 耐久消費財の需要が弱いことに加え、 当局が実施した需要喚起策の
効果が一巡していることも重なり、自動車（前年比 ▲21.2％）は引き続き大幅に下振れする一方、
新型スマートフォンの発売などを反映して家電（前年比＋1.9％）は前年を上回る伸びで推移して
いる。しかし、雇用不安などを理由とする家計部門の節約志向が根強く残るなかで宝飾品（前年比
▲9.6％）のほか、娯楽関連（同▲1.1％）の需要も前年を下回っている。飲食料品など生活必需品
や、 日用品に対する需要には底堅い動きがみられるものの、 これはＥＣを通じた購買が常態化して
いることが影響しているとみられる。 家計部門の節約志向が一段と強まるなかでオンライン取引に
おける価格競争は激化しており、 結果的にディスインフレ圧力を脱することができない一因となっ
ている可能性がある。 このように、 個人消費を巡る動きは一見持ち直しの様相をみせているものの、
その内実については引き続き厳しい状況にあると捉えられる。

4/6 
【不動産投資は依然底がみえない一方、設備投資や公共投資には底打ちの兆しがでている】 
不動産不況が家計部門の節約志向に加え、不動産需要の足かせとなるなか、地方政府レベルでは
需要喚起に向けた様々な対策が打ち出されている。 こうした動きを追い風に、 一部の大都市では持
ち直しの兆しがうかがえる一方、 多くの地方都市では下落の動きに歯止めがかからない状況が続い
ている。 ９月の新築住宅価格指数も前年
同月比▲2.8％と前月（同▲3.0％）から
マイナス幅は縮小したものの、 前月比は
▲0.1％と 下 落に 歯止め が かか ら ない
（図３） 。一部の１級都市では中古住宅
もわずかに上昇しており、 最悪期を脱し
つつある様子がうかがえる。一方で、２
級都市や３級都市など地方都市では、 新
築・中古問わず住宅価格の下落が続いて
おり、地域ごとに「まだら模様」状態に
ある。なお、大都市部の新築住宅価格が
上昇に転じた背景には、 不動産開発会社の資金調達環境の厳しさを理由に供給が絞られていること
が影響しており、需要拡大の動きが価格上昇を促している状況にはほど遠い。 
９月の不動産投資も年初来前年比▲20.6％と前月 （同▲19.9％） からマイナス幅が拡大しており、
過去に遡って最もマイナス幅が大きくなっている。 当研究所が試算した単月ベースの前年同月比の
伸びは▲26.0％と前月（同▲22.5％）からマイナス幅は拡大しており、引き続き歴史的低水準で推
移している。分野別でも、住宅、オフィス、商業用不動産のいずれも低迷しているほか、建設から
販売にかけてすべての段階で大幅なマイナスとなっている。 地域別では、 東北地区で大幅減少が続
いているほか、 それ以外の地域も低迷しており、 地方都市による支援拡充の動きにもかかわらず資
金流入も細っており、不動産市場は底がみえない状況にある。 
企業部門による設備投資や公共投資 などを含む９月の固定資産投資も年初来前年比 ▲7.2％と６
ヵ月連続のマイナスで推移しており、マ
イナス幅は前月 （同▲7.2％） から横ばい
で推移している。当研究所が試算した単
月ベースの前年同月比の伸びは ▲7.2％
と前月（同▲10.4％）からマイナス幅は
縮小しており、前月比も＋0.07％と前月
（同▲0.55％）から７ヵ月ぶりの拡大に
転じるなど持ち直しの兆しがみられる
（図４） 。前述の通り、 当局が公共投資の
進捗促進を図る動きをみせていること
が影響している可能性がある。 とはい

### Page 5

5/6 
え、 固定資産投資の規模は依然としてコロナ禍の影響が最も色濃く現れた時期をも下回っていると
試算されるなど、足元の状況は極めて厳しいことには変わりがない。

【中国景気は引き続き供給サイドがけん引役となる状況は変わらない】 
供給動向を示す９月の鉱工業生産は前年同月比＋5.5％となり、前月（同＋5.2％）から加速して
６ヵ月ぶりの高い伸びとなった。前月比も＋0.61％と前月（同＋0.54％）から拡大ペースが加速し
ており、持ち直しの動きを強めている様
子がうかがえるなど、足元においても供
給サイドが景気のけん引役となってい
る状況は変わらない（図５） 。前述の通
り、内需は消費、投資ともに持ち直しの
兆しがみられるものの、依然として力強
さを欠く推移をみせていることを勘案
すれば、中国国内では需給を巡る不均衡
状態が長期化している。したがって、中
国国内における余剰生産を解消する観
点でも、足元の景気は外需に依存せざる
を得ない状況が続いていると捉えられる。 
分野別では、 製造業 （前年比＋6.0％） の生産は引き続き堅調に推移している。 なかでも外需の堅
調さをけん引しているハイテク製造業（前年比＋15.3％）は高い伸びで推移しており、外需の堅調
さが生産活動を押し上げる展開が続いている。 また、 異常気象の頻発を受けてエネルギー需要が押
し上げられていることを反映して、 エネルギー関連 （前年比＋5.6％） も底堅い動きをみせている。
さらに、 原油をはじめとする商品市況が高止まりしていることを受けて、 国内供給を拡大させるべ
く鉱業 （前年比＋1.3％） も前年を上回る伸びに転じており、 資源調達の多様化を目指す動きが中国
国内の生産動向にも影響していると考えられる。 
財別では、 習近平指導部が主導する新質生産力のほか、 サプライチェーンの自立自強への取り組
みを反映して集積回路（前年比＋18.4％）や産業用ロボット（同＋18.9％）はともに高い伸びとな
るなど、旺盛な動きが続いている。一方で、需要喚起策の効果一巡を反映して、自動車（前年比
▲5.7％）のほか、マイコン（同▲17.0％） 、携帯電話（同▲26.9％）は軒並み減少するとともに、
マイナス幅も拡大している。 不動産需要の低迷が続くとともに、 供給も抑制されていることを反映
して、粗鋼（前年比＋0.1％）や銑鉄（同▲1.4％） 、鋼材（同▲3.8％）の生産は引き続き低迷して
おり、 板ガラス （同▲14.0％） やセメント （同▲5.3％） もマイナスとなるなど、 中国国内での過剰
生産能力の縮小に向けた取り組みも生産の足かせとなっている。 イラン情勢の不透明感を受けた原
油高が長期化するなか、 当局が石油製品の国内供給を優先して在庫が積み上がる一方、 精製マージ
ンは低迷しており、 石油精製量 （前年比▲8.4％） は低迷している。 さらに、 原油高を受けて世界的
に再生可能エネルギーの需要が高まっているものの、太陽光電池（前年比 ▲5.1％）や発電機（同
図 5 鉱工業生産(季節調整値)の推移

▲7.2％） の生産はともに低迷しており、 世界的に中国製品による 「デフレの輸出」 への警戒感が強
まっていることが影響しているとみられる。

【中国本土株は引き続き上値の重い展開をみせるなか、先行きはどうなるか】 
原油をはじめとする商品市況が高止まりするなか、中国においても企業部門はインフレに直面し
ている。 こうした状況にもかかわらず、 前述の通り企業部門は原材料価格の上昇を製品価格に転嫁
することが困難であり、 採算が圧迫され
る状況が続いている。こうしたことか
ら、 中国本土株は上値の重い展開が続い
てきた （図６） 。 そのうえ、 当局が不動産
開発に関する新規制を発表したことを
受けて、 不動産開発企業を取り巻く事業
環境が悪化するとの懸念が高まり、 関連
株を中心に下押し圧力がかかったこと
も相場を下押ししている。 足元ではイラ
ン情勢のさらなる長期化、 複雑化が懸念
されるなかで原油価格は高止まりして
おり、企業部門の事業環境は一段と厳しさを増す懸念が高まっている。先行きについては、当局に
よる政策の方向性が業績に影響する度合いが強まることが予想されることから、 分野ごとのばらつ
きが大きくなることに注意が必要である。

景気は弱い動き
中国景気は弱い動きが続いているが、改善の兆しもみられる。2026年7～9月期の実質GDP成長率は前年比＋4.5%
だった。前期（前年比＋4.3%）から伸びが拡大し、1～9月累計で同＋4.6%と政府目標の「4.5～5%」の範囲内で推移した。
ただし、10月の製造業PMIは49.3と前月から低下し、好不況の境目である50を2カ月ぶりに下回った。構成指数をみると、
生産と新規受注の低下が全体を押し下げた。非製造業PMIも49.7と低下し、50を下回った。

2026年7～9月期のGDP 外需の強さを背景に製造業が成長率を押し上げ
2026年7～9月期の実質GDP成長率は、前期から伸びが拡大した（1頁参照）。需要項目別にみると、総資本形成の増加
寄与が大きく縮小し、内需寄与度は低下したものの、輸出の大幅増加を背景に外需寄与度が拡大した（左図）。
業種別にみると、旺盛な外需に支えられ、製造業の伸びが高まったほか、前年同期の伸びが小さかった小売・卸売や宿
泊・飲食も増加幅の拡大が目立った（右図）。一方、不動産不況が長引く中、建設業は6四半期連続で、不動産業は5四半
期連続で前年割れとなった。

国慶節の人出は増加も、財布のひもは依然固い
中国の建国記念日である国慶節（10/1）に伴う大型連休（10/1～7）の1日当たり国内旅行者数は、前年比＋6.3%と増加
した（左図）。休日日数は前年より1日短かったものの、今年は中秋節の連休が9月末にあり、間で休暇を取れば最大13連

（右図）。旅行需要は底堅いものの、消費者の節約志向は依然として根強い。
